How am I to dress up in my finery and sit before complex feasts after the sorrow I’ve seen to-day?”

The dam broke, and Margaret poured out the grim reality of Boucher’s starving children. The story agitated Mrs. Hale excessively. Her distress demanded the immediate, tactile comfort of providing sustenance. In the drawing-room itself, she orchestrated the packing of a basket with a culinary historian’s care—crusted loaves of dense, fermented bread, sharp cheeses wrapped in muslin, and a rich, gelatinous bone broth meant to fortify the blood. She was nearly angry when Margaret gently suggested it could wait until morning, noting that Higgins had procured their immediate needs and she had left silver with Bessy. For Mrs. Hale, the act of feeding was not merely utilitarian; it was a sacred, mythic duty, and to delay was to deny her own agrarian identity. She allowed herself no breathing-time until the basket vanished into the Milton twilight.

Then she paused. “After all, we may have been doing wrong. Mr. Thornton said those were no true friends who helped to prolong the struggle by assisting the turn-outs. And this Boucher-man was a turn-out, was he not?”

The very word *turn-out* felt alien on her tongue—a harsh, industrial lexicon that stripped the humanity from a starving man. When Mr. Hale came upstairs, fresh from his lesson with Mr. Thornton, the question was laid before him. Margaret, her pulse still quickened, cared little for the abstract politics of prolonged strife; her mind was fixed on the visceral reality of empty stomachs. Mr. Hale listened, attempting to weigh the matter with judicial calm. He parsed Mr. Thornton’s logic: if the strike stretched on, the masters would simply import hands from afar, or invent some deafening new machinery to replace human touch entirely. To bolster their defiance might indeed be a cruel delay of the inevitable. Yet, Mr. Hale could not reconcile this sterile economic vocabulary with his own moral lexicon. He compromised: his wife and daughter had acted righteously, but he would visit Boucher himself in the morning to see what deeper remedy might be applied.

The next morning, the visit yielded a curiously brightened report. Boucher was out, but Mr. Hale had spoken deeply with his wife, securing an Infirmary order. He marveled at the Hale basket’s bounty, which the children had devoured with a ravenous, untutored joy. His relief painted the scene with an imaginative gloss. “But I will go again,” Mr. Hale noted. “I hardly know how to translate their world into our Helstone understanding. I see parlor furniture our southern laborers would never dream of, and cuts of meat they would deem high luxury; yet without their weekly wages, this entire domestic ecosystem collapses into the pawn-shop. We must learn an entirely new vocabulary of survival up here.”

Bessy was slightly stronger that day, but the feverish desire to see Margaret in her evening finery had evaporated from her mind. Margaret prepared for the Thornton dinner with a heavy heart, the impending feast feeling entirely disconnected from the raw hunger she had just touched. She found solace only in the joy it brought her mother. Her toilette was a ritual echoing a distant past. Dixon, serving as the proud curator of Margaret’s appearance, twisted her thick, raven hair into massive, silken coils, anchoring the weight with two long coral pins that resembled ancient arrows. Heavy coral beads rested against the base of her milk-white throat, their vivid color striking against the white silk sleeves.

“Oh, Margaret! How I should like to be going with you to one of the old Barrington assemblies,” Mrs. Hale sighed, a ghost of a smile touching her lips. 

Margaret kissed her, though her spirit felt thoroughly untranslatable to the festive mood. “I would rather stay at home with you, mamma.”

“Nonsense, darling! Be sure you notice the dinner well. I want to hear how they manage their kitchens in Milton. Note the second course—see what they serve in place of game.”

Had Mrs. Hale been present, she would have been astounded by the sheer, aggressive opulence of the Thornton table. Margaret, possessing a deeply cultivated London palate, found the culinary landscape almost oppressive in its volume. There were brilliantly executed dishes—a slow-braised venison glazed with a dark, juniper reduction; intricately larded poultry emitting the volatile oils of rosemary and citrus; spun-sugar towers that defied gravity—yet the overabundance drowned the nuanced flavor profiles. It was a core tenet of Mrs. Thornton’s hospitality: a mountainous, unyielding provision. Careless to abstemiousness in her own spartan habits, the matriarch used elaborate gastronomy as an expression of absolute power. Her son shared this ethos; to him, grand society was synonymous with an exchange of superb, meticulously sourced meals, a tangible proof of industrial triumph over the earth’s raw ingredients.

Margaret and her father arrived meticulously on time. In the drawing-room, swathed in yellow silk damask, every corner was choked with ornament, a stark, commercialized beauty that felt utterly alien to the bald, soot-stained reality of the mill-yard visible through the windows. The great factory loomed, casting an unnaturally early twilight over the room. 

“My son was engaged up to the last moment on business. He will be here directly,” Mrs. Thornton announced. 

Looking out the window, Mr. Hale asked gently, “Don’t you find such close neighborhood to the mill rather unpleasant at times?”

Mrs. Thornton drew herself up, her identity rooted in the industrial soil. “Never. I am not become so fine as to desire to forget the source of my son’s wealth. There is not such another factory in Milton. One room alone is two hundred and twenty square yards.”

“I meant the smoke, and the noise,” Mr. Hale clarified. 

“I agree with you, Mr. Hale!” Fanny chimed in, adjusting her silks. “There is a continual smell of steam and oily machinery, and the noise is perfectly deafening.”

Margaret silently agreed with Fanny; the relentless, rhythmic thrumming of the engines was a catastrophic acoustic pollution, a violent sonic environment that disrupted the delicate frequencies of human thought. 

But Mrs. Thornton’s eyes flashed. “I have heard noise that was called music far more deafening. The continuous murmur of the workpeople disturbs me no more than the organic humming of a hive. It is the sound of my son’s intellect made manifest. Just now, it is quiet; the hands have been ungrateful enough to turn out. But the business he attends to now is to make them learn their place.” 

When Mr. Thornton entered, the dark anger on his mother’s face held fast, though he masked his profound exhaustion behind a cordial greeting. He took Margaret’s hand—their first physical contact, though she remained perfectly unconscious of the milestone. He inquired of Mrs. Hale, his eyes scanning Margaret’s face to read the subtle dialects of her expression. Finding no dissent, he was struck anew by her staggering beauty. The elegance of her attire, the smooth ivory of her shoulder, and the serene, unbroken line of her posture stood in sharp contrast to his sister Fanny’s restless, unmoored fidgeting. Thornton sighed, absorbing the quiet power of Margaret’s stillness in one comprehensive glance, before turning his full, forceful intellect to Mr. Hale.

At dinner, Margaret found herself fascinated by the men’s discourse. The Milton manufacturers spoke a dialect of raw creation and commerce, their words vibrating with desperate earnestness. She recognized the local idioms, the technical jargon of the looms, and the complex supply chains of their trade. They debated warmly until Mr. Thornton spoke. His voice carried the resonant authority of a man who required no artificial dominance; his explanations of trade dynamics were so precise and structurally sound that opposition melted away. Margaret saw him then not as the vexed, defensive pupil from her parlor, but as a master of his domain, rooted in a quiet, grand security. 

Yet, as the rich sauces were cleared and the air grew thick with the scent of roasted coffee and the sweet decay of overripe fruit, she noted the utter absence of the strike in their conversation. The masters viewed the starvation of the workers through a lexicon devoid of empathy—the "hands" were simply cutting their own throats by following paid delegates. They chuckled inwardly at the thought of Thornton crushing the rebellion, viewing him as a man of iron who would not yield an iota of his decree.

Later, when the gentlemen rejoined the ladies, Margaret welcomed the interruption of the women’s petty, superficial critiques of dress. She gravitated toward the men’s exultant energy. While she critiqued their rampant boasting and their desire to conquer all inanimate matter, she admired their forward momentum—a sweeping, multi-generational effort to reshape the earth, even if it lacked the softer, acoustic harmonies of the south.

Mr. Thornton appeared at her elbow. “I could see you were on our side in our discussion at dinner—were you not, Miss Hale?”

“Certainly. But I know so little about it. I was surprised to find from Mr. Horsfall that others thought so diametrically opposite. The Mr. Morison he spoke of—he cannot be a gentleman, is he?”

“I am not quite the person to decide on another’s gentlemanliness, Miss Hale. I don’t quite understand your application of the word. I should say that this Morison is no true man.”

“I suspect my ‘gentleman’ includes your ‘true man,’” Margaret countered, feeling the friction of their conflicting lexicons.

“And a great deal more, you would imply. I differ from you. A man is to me a higher and a completer being than a gentleman.”

“What do you mean? We must understand the words differently.” She acutely felt the inherent untranslatability of her southern, historically rich dialect against his stark, industrial pragmatism.

“I take it that ‘gentleman’ is a term that only describes a person in his relation to others,” Thornton explained, dismantling the old vocabulary. “But when we speak of him as ‘a man,’ we consider him in relation to himself—to life, to time, to eternity. A castaway lonely as Robinson Crusoe, a prisoner immured in a dungeon, has his strength best described by being spoken of as ‘a man.’ I am weary of this word ‘gentlemanly,’ which is often inappropriately used with exaggerated distortion of meaning. The full, unadorned simplicity of ‘man’ is acknowledged; the other I class with the cant of the day.”

Margaret paused, her mind slowly turning over the profound power dynamics embedded in his rejection of her native tongue. But before she could articulate her conviction, Thornton was pulled away by the eager manufacturers. 

She could not hear their words, only the acoustic footprint of Thornton’s replies, which came steady and rhythmic, echoing like the boom of a distant minute gun. They were dissecting the strike. 

“I take the risk,” Thornton’s voice rose above the murmur. “You need not join in it unless you choose. I am not afraid of anything so dastardly as incendiarism. We are open enemies; and I can protect myself. And I will assuredly protect all others who come to me for work. They know my determination.”

Across the room, the dominant commercial world continued its careless consumption of the domestic. Mr. Horsfall pulled Thornton aside to inquire not of trade, but of the quiet, stately beauty from the south. 

“From Hampshire, I believe,” was Thornton’s cold, indifferent deflection.

Nearby, Mrs. Slickson catechized Fanny about Margaret’s father. 

“Oh dear, no! That is Mr. Hale. He gives lessons; he reads with young men,” Fanny explained, her voice tinged with the anxiety of one who fears the judgment of the mercantile elite. “My brother John goes to him twice a week.”

“Mr. Thornton! Does he really find time to read with a tutor, in the midst of all his business—and this abominable strike in hand as well?” 

Fanny blushed, caught between the ancient, profound pursuit of classical knowledge and the harsh, immediate reality of Milton’s industrial noise, a quiet shame lingering in the air as the guests finally began to disperse.

Margaret and her father walked home through the labyrinth of Milton’s streets. The night air was cool, though it carried none of the restorative aromas of their Southern past—no damp loam, no crushed wild garlic, nor the slow, sweet woodsmoke of ancestral hearths. Instead, the olfactory landscape was coated in the sterile, burnt-sugar taint of the mills. Yet Margaret, her pretty white silk kilted up to her knee like Leezie Lindsay’s gown in the old ballads, felt a surge of physical vitality, ready to dance along with the sharp bite of the evening. 

“I rather think Thornton is not quite easy in his mind about this strike,” Mr. Hale said, his voice a low, melodic baritone that momentarily humanized the oppressive acoustic ecology of the industrial streets. “He seemed very anxious to-night.”

“I should wonder if he were not,” Margaret replied. “But he spoke with his usual coolness to the others, when they suggested different things, just before we came away.”

“So he did after dinner as well. It would take a good deal to stir him from his cool manner of speaking; but his face strikes me as anxious.”

“I should be, if I were he. He must know of the growing anger and hardly smothered hatred of his workpeople, who all looked upon him as what the Bible calls a ‘hard man’—not so much unjust as unfeeling. He stands upon his ‘rights’ as no human being ought to stand, using the dominant lexicon of commerce to justify himself, while entirely erasing the ancient, communal dialect of sustenance and mutual care. I am glad you think he looks anxious. When I remember Boucher’s half-mad words and ways, the raw, untranslatable vernacular of his hunger, I cannot bear to think how coolly Mr. Thornton spoke.”

“In the first place,” Mr. Hale countered, “I am not so convinced as you are about that man Boucher’s utter distress. But from what you said, it was evident the man was of a passionate, demonstrative nature, and gave strong expression to all he felt.”

“Oh, papa!”

“Well! I only want you to do justice to Mr. Thornton, who is, I suspect, of an exactly opposite nature—a man who is far too proud to show his feelings. Just the character I should have thought beforehand you would have admired, Margaret.”

“So I do—so I should; but I don’t feel quite so sure as you do of the existence of those feelings. He is a man of great strength of character, but his worldview is so terribly mechanized.”

“He has led a practical life from a very early age,” Mr. Hale said gently. “To be sure, he needs some of the knowledge of the past, which gives the truest basis for conjecture as to the future; but he knows this need. You are quite prejudiced against Mr. Thornton, Margaret.”

“He is my first olive, papa: let me make a face while I swallow it. I know he is good of his kind, and by and by I shall like the kind. But I was so terribly disappointed by the women to-night. When Miss Thornton took me away to the drawing-room, I found their company utterly bereft of sensory appreciation. They possessed no reverence for the sustenance they had just consumed. The clear consommé, the heavily spiced imported meats—they ate without tasting the soil, without acknowledging the meticulous culinary techniques or the agrarian hands that harvested the ingredients. They viewed the feast solely as utilitarian fuel and a display of commercial triumph.”

“What do you mean, child?” asked Mr. Hale.

“They spoke a sterile language of status,” Margaret explained, her frustration mounting at the erosion of true cultural memory. “They took nouns that were signs of things which gave evidence of wealth—housekeepers, under-gardeners, extent of glass, valuable lace, diamonds—and each one formed her speech to bring them all in. It reminded me of a parlor game. There was no talk of the changing seasons, no sharing of preserving techniques or ancestral recipes that bind a family to its past. I felt like a great hypocrite, sitting there in my white silk gown with my idle hands, when I remembered all the good, thorough, domestic work they had done to-day to prepare. They took me for a fine lady, entirely divorced from the physical realities of the hearth.”

“Even I was mistaken enough to think you looked like a lady, my dear,” said Mr. Hale, quietly smiling.

But those smiles evaporated into white, trembling dread the moment Dixon opened their front door. The acoustic environment of the house had shifted entirely; the comforting, rhythmic domestic sounds were gone, replaced by a vacuum of hushed terror. 

“Oh, master!—Oh, Miss Margaret! Thank God you are come!” Dixon gasped, her usually steadfast, traditional Southern composure shattered. “Dr. Donaldson is here. The servant next door went for him. She’s better now; but, oh sir! I thought she’d have died an hour ago.”

Mr. Hale caught Margaret’s arm. His face contracted not with the agony of terror, but with an unprepared, visceral shock. Margaret, however, listened with a hopeless expression of awed apprehension. She knew more than he did; the mythic shadow of mortality had been weaving itself into their daily routines for weeks, an unseen guest lingering by their dining table. 

“Oh! I should not have left her—wicked daughter that I am!” Margaret moaned, supporting her father’s tottering steps upstairs. 

Dr. Donaldson met them on the landing, carrying with him the sharp, bitter scent of medicinal tinctures—a smell of macerated poppy and distilled earthy roots that harshly overwhelmed the house’s usual olfactory warmth. “She is better now,” he whispered into the fraught silence. “The opiate has taken effect. The spasms were very bad: no wonder they frightened your maid; but she’ll rally this time.”

*This time!* Half an hour ago Mr. Hale was a hale, middle-aged man. Now his sight was dim, his senses wavering, stepping into the bedroom as if he were seventy. 

There lay Mrs. Hale. The ancient, mythic presence of Death had visibly signed her for his own, resting upon her brow as naturally as the evening shadows in the room. Mr. Hale stared at his wife, his entire frame beginning to shake. Turning blindly from the doctor, he groped for the door he could no longer see. He staggered into the drawing-room, collapsing into a chair Dr. Donaldson wheeled forward.

“Speak to him, Miss Hale. We must rouse him.”

“Papa!” Margaret cried, her voice wild with an untranslatable pain. “Papa! Speak to me!”

He made a great effort. “Margaret, did you know of this? Oh, it was cruel of you!”

“No, sir, it was not cruel!” replied Dr. Donaldson with quick, clinical decision. “Miss Hale acted under my directions. She has taken the opiate I brought; she will have a good long sleep, and to-morrow that look which has alarmed you so much will have passed away.”

“But not the disease?” 

Dr. Donaldson glanced at Margaret’s bent head. He saw no appeal for a false reprieve. “Not the disease. We cannot touch the disease, with all our poor vaunted skill. We can only delay its progress—alleviate the pain. Be a man, sir—a Christian.”

The only reply was Mr. Hale’s choked words, “You have never been married, Dr. Donaldson; you don’t know what it is.” Then came his deep, manly sobs, breaking through the stillness of the night like heavy pulses of agony. It was a primal, acoustic expression of grief, a profound subjective experience that no dominant lexicon could ever capture or console. Margaret knelt by him, caressing him with tear-stained hands. 

Time became a circular, fluid entity. Dr. Donaldson gave his sensible directions, prescribing peace for the immediate morrow but no enduring hope, before leaving them to the quiet of the night. Mr. Hale was prevailed upon to rest on the drawing-room sofa. Dixon, after stoutly refusing to leave, finally succumbed to exhaustion, her snores providing a rhythmic, organic baseline that contrasted with the oppressive silence. 

Margaret discarded her silk gown with impatient disgust, wrapping herself in her dressing-gown. Sleep was impossible; her senses were acutely vital. She sat behind the curtain, a meticulous historian of her own fading life. Far away in time and space seemed the events of thirty-six hours ago. Her concern for Bessy Higgins, her heart wrung for Boucher’s starvation—it all felt like a dreaming memory. 

In the quiet, Helstone returned to her, not as a distant geography, but as an immediate sensory haunting. The dull gray days of the preceding winter, the smell of roasted root vegetables, the seasonal rhythms of the foraging harvest, the meticulous, slow-simmered broths her mother once presided over—these agrarian memories felt more fiercely real than the soot-stained windows of Milton. She mourned the erosion of that cultural memory, the slow death of the native dialect of her childhood. What a vain show Life seemed in this industrial North! It was as if from some aerial belfry, high above the harsh, mechanized noise pollution of the earth, a bell tolled: *All are shadows!—all are passing!—all is past!*

When morning dawned, cool and gray, the terrible night felt as unreal as a dream. Mrs. Hale awoke unaware of how close she had brushed against the veil. She consented to remain in bed that day, but by the next, the heavy opiate’s reaction left her restless, feverish, and deeply uncomfortable. 

On the third day, Dr. Donaldson analyzed her distress. “It is the reaction after the powerful opiates. I think if we could get a water-bed it might be a good thing to prevent the physical toll on her skin. Mrs. Thornton has one, I know. It would do you no harm to have a brisk walk to Marlborough Street, Miss Hale, and ask if she can spare it.”

“Certainly,” Margaret agreed. “I could go while mamma is asleep.”

Mrs. Hale seemed brighter that afternoon after a small, carefully prepared broth, her hand lying in her husband’s. Margaret left them and stepped out into the crushing August heat. 

The sun beat straight down onto the Milton cobbles, baking the dust until the air tasted of hot iron and stale ash. Margaret walked the first mile absorbed in her impending loss, threading through the irregular stream of human beings. But gradually, her finely tuned acoustic awareness registered a profound disruption in the city’s ecology.

Usually, the oppressive, relentless violence of the power-looms dictated the psychological weather of Milton—a mechanical noise pollution that commodified the very air. Today, that rhythmic industrial heartbeat was missing. In its place was a low, distant roar, as of myriads of fierce indignant voices. The inhabitants of the squalid dwellings were gathered in the narrow ways, buzzing with a restless, oppressive irritation. 

From every lane opening onto Marlborough Street came the sound of a gathering tempest. It was the chaotic symphony of the local dialect, an untranslatable wave of acoustic fury rising from the eroded, displaced agrarian souls of the workers. Their voices—fierce, dilated with fear, relentless with threat—surged together. As Margaret reached the small side-entrance by the folding doors in the great dead wall of Marlborough mill-yard, she heard the first slow-surging wave of the dark crowd tumble over and retreat. 

The circumstances pressed upon her senses, but her heart, occupied solely by the deep, physical ache of her mother's fading life, could not process the mob’s significance. The porter opened the door cautiously, his face pale, widening the entrance just enough to admit her before hastily bolting it shut.

“Th’ folk are all coming up here, I reckon?” he breathed heavily.

“I don’t know,” Margaret said. “Something unusual seemed going on.”

She went across the yard. The acoustic vacuum within the walls was terrifying. There was no near sound—no steam engine at work with beat and pant, no click of machinery, no mingling of sharp commercial voices. There was only, from far away, the ominous, deep-clamoring roar of the tempest, asserting its ancient, untranslatable power against the silent mill.

Margaret was shown into the Thornton drawing-room, a space returned to its normal state of shrouded furniture and stifled air. The windows were cracked open against the oppressive heat, yet the Venetian blinds severely sliced the light, casting a grim, gray pallor reflected from the baking pavement below. The room lacked the rich, yeasty aromas of a lived-in hearth; it smelled only of sterile dust and preserved linens. As Margaret caught her reflection in the green-tinged mirrors, her face looked ghastly, drained of the vibrant, sun-warmed flush of her Southern agrarian youth. She sat in the heavy silence. The local acoustic ecology felt unnaturally pressurized, a profound stillness that was periodically bruised by a distant, multitudinous vibration—a sweeping wave of sound that carried no wind, but tasted faintly of metallic ash and sour desperation on the back of the tongue. 

Fanny entered at last, her shallow footsteps a grating intrusion upon the quiet. She was a creature who viewed the profound rituals of sustenance as mere utilitarian fuel, her sensibilities entirely divorced from the earth. 

“Mamma will come directly, Miss Hale,” she said, her voice a thin, unevocative instrument. “She desired me to apologise. Perhaps you know my brother has imported hands from Ireland, and it has irritated the Milton people excessively. As if he hadn’t a right to get labour where he could! Now the stupid wretches here have frightened these poor Irish starvelings so with their threats, that we daren’t let them out. You may see them huddled in that top room in the mill. Mamma is seeing about their food, and John is speaking to them, for some of the women are crying to go back to their own land. Ah! here’s mamma!”

Mrs. Thornton swept in. Though her face was set in black sternness, Margaret sensed in her the ancestral weight of the traditional matriarch—a woman who understood the severe, elemental duty of provisioning a household against the famine of the world. It was a bad time to ask for favors, yet Margaret, speaking in the soft, rhythmic cadence of her native Hampshire dialect, gently requested a water-bed to ease the physical suffering of her mother’s illness. 

Mrs. Thornton did not reply. Instead, the older woman started up, her brow suddenly contracting. “They’re at the gates! Call John, Fanny,—call him in from the mill! They’ll batter them in!”

Simultaneously, the acoustic equilibrium of the street was violently ruptured. The gathering tramp Margaret had sensed was now a concussive blast right outside the wall. An increasing din of angry voices raged against the wooden barrier. It was a terrifying industrial noise pollution, the sheer volume of starving bodies throwing themselves like rhythmic battering rams against the timber, making the strong gates quiver like wheat stalks before a gale. 

The women gathered around the windows, physically drawn to the terrifying spectacle. Fanny had already fled upstairs with hysterical shrieks, but Mrs. Thornton, the women-servants, and Margaret remained. Mr. Thornton emerged from the mill. He looked up at their pale faces, offering a steady smile of good courage, before locking the factory door. His mother went down to unbar the house door for him. When the infuriated multitude heard the sharp, commanding baritone of his voice—the dominant lexicon of the master echoing across the yard—it was like the taste of raw blood to a ravening pack. Hitherto, they had been voiceless, hoarding their breath for the physical labor of breaking the gates. Now, they released a fierce, unearthly groan, a sound entirely stripped of the rich, regional dialect of Milton, reduced to the untranslatable, guttural frequency of mass starvation. 

Mr. Thornton entered the room flushed, smelling sharply of machine oil, raw cotton, and the metallic tang of adrenaline. His eyes gleamed with a proud defiance that rendered him suddenly, mythically noble. Margaret, who had always feared a lack of sensory fortitude within herself, felt only an intense, vibrating sympathy—a piercing alignment with the raw, beating pulse of the moment.

“I’m sorry, Miss Hale, you have visited us at this unfortunate moment,” Mr. Thornton said frankly. “Mother! hadn’t you better go into the back rooms? Go, Jane!” he commanded the upper-servant, who fled with the others. 

“I stop here!” his mother declared, her voice rooted in the deep soil of maternal endurance. “Where you are, there I stay.” 

The servants’ shrieks echoed from the garrets, a superficial panic Mr. Thornton met with a scornful smile. He glanced at Margaret, who stood solitary by the window. Her eyes glittered, a deep, vital color rushing back into her cheeks and lips. Feeling his gaze, she asked a question that touched upon the very ethics of sustenance: “Where are the poor imported workpeople? In the factory there?”

“Yes! I left them cowed up in a small room at the head of a back flight of stairs. But it is not them—it is me they want.”

“When can the soldiers be here?” his mother asked, her tone low but steady.

Thornton pulled out his watch with meticulous composure. “Supposing Williams got straight off... it must be twenty minutes yet.”

“Twenty minutes!” The sudden terror in his mother’s voice was palpable, a crack in her formidable architecture. 

“Shut down the windows instantly, mother,” he exclaimed. “The gates won’t bear such another shock. Shut down that window, Miss Hale.”

Margaret obeyed, moving to assist Mrs. Thornton’s trembling fingers. By shutting the glass, they muted the violent roar into a heavy, suffocating hum. For several minutes, a pregnant pause hung over the unseen street. Mrs. Thornton watched her son’s rigid face for translation of the silence. 

“Are they gone?” Fanny whispered, having raised herself up.

“Gone!” replied he. “Listen!”

Through the glass came the straining breath of the mob, the organic creak of yielding wood, the violent wrench of iron, and the mighty, thunderous collapse of the ponderous gates. Fanny tottered and fell forward into a dead faint. Mrs. Thornton, drawing on a well of sheer, bodily will, lifted her daughter and carried her away.

“Thank God!” Mr. Thornton murmured. “Had you not better go upstairs, Miss Hale?”

Margaret’s lips formed a silent “No!” Her voice was entirely drowned by the tramp of innumerable steps directly beneath the wall. The low, deep growl of the men possessed a ferocious, gustatory satisfaction now that the barrier was breached—a sound far more dreadful than their baffled cries. 

“Never mind,” he said, attempting reassurance. “It cannot last long now; a few minutes more, and the soldiers will be here.”

“Oh, God!” Margaret cried, her eyes locking onto a figure below. “There is Boucher. I know his face, though he is livid with rage—look! look!” 

She recognized Boucher from the deep, agrarian hollows of his cheeks, a man whose family was starving, denied the fundamental right of daily bread. To the mob, the sight of Mr. Thornton at the window provoked a yell that transcended human language—it was the demoniac desire of a wild beast for the sustenance withheld from its ravening maw. Even Thornton drew back, dismayed by the sheer olfactory and acoustic intensity of the hatred he had fermented.

“Let them yell!” he said. “I only hope my poor Irishmen are not terrified out of their wits. Keep up your courage for five minutes, Miss Hale.”

“Don’t be afraid for me,” she said hastily. “But what in five minutes? Can you do nothing to soothe these poor creatures? It is awful to see them.”

“The soldiers will be here directly, and that will bring them to reason.”

“To reason!” Margaret countered quickly, deeply critical of this sterile logic. “What kind of reason?”

“The only reason that does with men that make themselves into wild beasts. By heaven! they’ve turned to the mill-door!”

Margaret shook with a sudden, passionate fermentation of spirit. “Mr. Thornton, go down this instant, if you are not a coward. Go down and face them like a man. Save these poor strangers, whom you have decoyed here. Speak to your workmen as if they were human beings. Speak to them kindly. Don’t let the soldiers come in and cut down poor creatures who are driven mad. If you have any courage or noble quality in you, go out and speak to them, man to man!”

He turned, a dark cloud passing over his features. The dominant lexicon of his authority clashed with her profound demand for human empathy. He set his teeth. “I will go. Perhaps I may ask you to accompany me downstairs, and bar the door behind me; my mother and sister will need that protection.”

He was gone before her dizzy head could process the shift. She followed him down, sliding the great iron bar into place with a heavy clank, then clambered back up to the farthest window. He stood on the steps below. She threw the window wide, letting the raw, unfiltered sonic environment wash over her. 

Many in the crowd were gaunt as wolves, their stomachs hollowed by the profound absence of nourishment, enraged that Irish laborers were brought in to rob their little ones of bread. Margaret understood this multi-generational trauma; she read it in Boucher’s desperate face. If only Thornton would speak to them, use a shared dialect to bridge the chasm. But he stood with his arms folded, pale and silent as a statue, offering only stony, unyielding silence against their raging.

The crowd’s noise swelled, an inarticulate roar devoid of cultural memory. Margaret felt intuitively that the first touch of violence would spark an explosion. In the background, she saw lads stooping to remove their heavy wooden clogs—shoes carved from the earth, worn through years of labor, now turned to readiest missiles. With a cry that no one heard, she rushed from the room. 

She flew downstairs, lifted the great iron bar with imperious force, and threw the door open wide. She stood facing the angry sea of men, the sour, fermented stench of their unwashed, starving bodies hitting her like a physical blow. The clogs were arrested in mid-air. For a moment, the visual shock of a woman inserting herself into this ancient, mythic clash of power held them irresolute. She tried to speak, to translate the profound subjective experience of her empathy into their dialect, but she had no breath. 

“Oh, do not use violence! He is one man, and you are many,” she whispered hoarsely. 

Mr. Thornton moved slightly, stepping away from her shelter, fiercely jealous of anything coming between him and the physical reality of danger. 

“Go!” she cried, her voice finally tearing through her dry throat. “The soldiers are sent for—are coming. Go peaceably. You shall have relief from your complaints.”

“Shall them Irish blackguards be packed back again?” yelled a voice from the crowd, a coarse assertion of their threatened lexicon.

“Never, for your bidding!” Mr. Thornton thundered back. 

Instantly, the storm broke. The acoustic assault of their hooting filled the air. Margaret saw the lads with the clogs read their aim. She thought only of how to save him, to shield the physical vessel of this man she had goaded into peril. She threw her arms around him, pressing her body against his chest, becoming a living barrier against the fierce populace. 

“Go away,” he rumbled in his deep voice, trying to shake her off. “This is no place for you.”

“It is,” she insisted. “You did not see what I saw.” 

She knew her sex offered no true protection against the reckless passion of starved men. A clog whizzed violently through the air, carrying the heavy scent of damp earth and sweat. It missed. Margaret turned sick with fright, burying her face against Mr. Thornton’s arm, but she did not retreat. 

“For God’s sake! do not damage your cause by this violence,” she strove to make her words distinct above the din.

A sharp pebble flew past, violently grazing her forehead and cheek. A blinding sheet of white light flashed across her vision, followed immediately by the hot, metallic taste of copper in the back of her throat. She collapsed like one dead against Mr. Thornton’s shoulder. 

He unfolded his arms and caught her in a firm embrace. “You do well!” he roared, his voice cutting through the industrial noise like a blade. “You come to oust the innocent stranger. You fall—you hundreds—on one man; and when a woman comes before you, to ask you for your own sakes to be reasonable creatures, your cowardly wrath falls upon her! You do well!”

The crowd fell silent, a sudden and profound acoustic void. They stared, open-mouthed, at the thread of dark-red blood stark against her pale skin. The shedding of blood—an ancient, visceral occurrence—snapped them from their trance. A retreating movement rippled through the mass.

Only one voice, clinging to the fading cultural memory of its native dialect, called out: “Th’ stone was meant for thee; but thou wert sheltered behind a woman!”

Thornton quivered with rage. He placed Margaret gently on the door-step. “Can you rest there?” he asked, and then walked slowly down into the very center of the mob. “Now kill me, if it is your brutal will. There is no woman to shield me here. You may beat me to death—you will never move me from what I have determined upon!”

But the retreat had already begun, driven by the distant, rhythmic clank of approaching soldiers and the sobering sight of Margaret’s marble-still face, her eyelashes wet with tears and the slower, heavier drip of her own blood. Even Boucher scowled and faltered away. When the retreat turned into a full flight, Thornton darted back up the steps. 

Margaret tried to rise, offering a sickly smile. “It is nothing. The skin is grazed... Oh, I am so thankful they are gone!” She wept without restraint, releasing the immense psychological pressure of the siege. 

Thornton could not yet sympathize; the adrenaline of unspent anger still boiled within his veins. He hoped the soldiers would quell the vanished mob. As Margaret clung to the doorpost, a gray film veiled her eyes, her senses failing. He caught her just in time. 

“Mother—mother!” he cried, bearing her into the dining-room. He laid her softly on the sofa, enveloped suddenly by the delicate scent of her hair and the stark, physical reality of her vulnerability. The untranslatable depth of his feeling rushed to the surface, bypassing all rational lexicon. 

“Oh, my Margaret—my Margaret! no one can tell what you are to me! Dead—cold as you lie there, you are the only woman I ever loved! Oh, Margaret—Margaret!” 

He moaned the words inarticulately, starting up in shame as his mother entered the room. Mrs. Thornton saw only her son, paler and sterner than usual. 

“Miss Hale is hurt, mother. A stone has grazed her temple.”

“She looks very seriously hurt,” Mrs. Thornton said, alarmed. 

“It is only a fainting-fit.” His entire body trembled as the blood rushed inward to his heart. “Go and call Jane. And do you go to your Irish people, who are crying and shouting as if they were mad with fright.”

He went to the Irish workers, every nerve thrilling with the sensory memory of Margaret’s embrace. He found the laborers weeping, speaking rapidly in a native tongue that was dying under the weight of English industry, demanding to be sent back to their agrarian homes. Thornton was forced to stay, to parse their dialect, to think, and to reason with their terror.

In the dining-room, Mrs. Thornton bathed Margaret’s temples with eau de Cologne. The distilled spirit was a sharp, volatile contrast to the soothing herbal broths of a traditional sickroom. As the piercing liquid touched the open wound, Margaret’s eyes fluttered. She was trapped in an untranslatable subjective space, unable to recognize the room or the faces above her. Her lips contracted, and she slipped back into insensibility. 

“Is there any one who will go for a doctor?” Mrs. Thornton demanded.

Jane shrank back. “Not me, ma’am... Them rabble may be all about.”

“If you are a coward, Jane, I am not. I will go.” Mrs. Thornton refused to let the servants' fear dictate the survival of her household. After commanding Jane to continue bathing Margaret’s head, she marched upstairs to rouse Fanny from her bed. 

“Nonsense! The men are all gone away,” Mrs. Thornton wrenched her gown from Fanny’s clinging hands. “Miss Hale is badly hurt. Go down to her, Fanny, and do try to make yourself of use.”

Fanny crept into the dining-room, terrified by the very notion of physical wounds. “Oh, Jane! What is the matter? How white she looks!”

Margaret’s senses were dimly returning. The sickly daze of the swoon left her trapped in a state of acoustic and sensory paralysis. She felt the cooling refreshment of the eau de Cologne, her body craving the continued physical sensation, yet she remained utterly mute. She was like one entombed in a death-like trance, fully aware of the subtle power dynamics in the hushed voices above her, yet incapable of accessing the dominant lexicon to speak.

Jane paused her ministrations to answer Fanny’s whispered questions, her tone laced with the quiet, subversive authority of servant gossip. 

“She’d have been safe enough, miss, if she’d stayed in the drawing-room. We were in the front garret, out of harm’s way.”

“Where was she then?” Fanny asked, creeping nearer. 

“Just before the front door—with master!” 

“With John! How did she get there?”

“Nay, miss, that’s not for me to say,” Jane tossed her head. “Sarah did—”

“Sarah what?” Fanny demanded sharply. 

Jane resumed bathing Margaret’s brow, deliberately withholding the information, relishing the control over the narrative. “Well, miss, since you will have it—Sarah was at the right-hand window; and she says she saw Miss Hale with her arms about master’s neck, hugging him before all the people.”

Margaret had not been gone five minutes when Mr. Thornton crossed the threshold into the dining room, his face suffused with the heat of a sudden, volatile reaction, a deep, roasted flush of adrenaline and awe. 

“I could not come sooner: the superintendent would—— Where is she?” He looked round the dining-room, seeking the physical anchor of her presence, and then turned almost fiercely at his mother. Mrs. Thornton was quietly re-arranging the disturbed furniture, meticulously attempting to restore the domestic acoustic ecology of the room, smoothing away the chaotic, echoing vibrations of the afternoon’s industrial violence. She did not instantly reply. 

“Where is Miss Hale?” asked he again, his voice cutting through the delicate clink of porcelain.

“Gone home,” said she, rather shortly, closing a drawer with a blunt wooden thud. 

“Gone home!” 

“Yes. She was a great deal better. Indeed, I don’t believe it was so very much of a hurt; only some people faint at the least thing.” 

“I am sorry she has gone home,” said he, walking uneasily about, his heavy footfalls disrupting the rhythmic, ticking quiet she was trying to cultivate. “She could not have been fit for it.” 

“She said she was; and Mr. Lowe said she was. I went for him myself.” 

“Thank you, mother.” He stopped, and partly held out his hand to give her a grateful shake. But she did not notice the movement, her attention fixed on the microscopic imperfections of her domain. 

“What have you done with your Irish people?” 

“Sent to the Dragon, for a good meal for them, poor wretches,” Thornton replied, instinctively understanding that profound psychological tremors required the deep, grounding weight of ancestral sustenance. “Thick cuts of slow-roasted mutton and a heavy, wild-yeast bread to quiet their panicked blood. And then, luckily, I caught Father Grady, and I’ve asked him in to speak to them. He is wrapping their fears in the rhythmic, ancient cadences of their native Gaelic—a linguistic poultice that holds the memory of their lost soil—to dissuade them from going off in a body. How did Miss Hale go home? I’m sure she could not walk.” 

“She had a cab. Everything was done properly, even to the paying. Let us talk of something else. She has caused disturbance enough.” 

“I don’t know where I should have been but for her.” 

“Are you become so helpless as to have to be defended by a girl?” asked Mrs. Thornton scornfully. Her dominant lexicon, rigid and unyielding, sought to reduce a mythic, near-sacrificial intercession into something small and foolish. 

He reddened, the color rising like the slow heat of a hearth fire. “Not many girls would have taken the blows on herself which were meant for me;—meant with right down goodwill, too.” 

“A girl in love will do a good deal,” replied Mrs. Thornton, shortly, translating an untranslatable moment of raw human solidarity into the shallow terminology of parlor romance.

“Mother!” He made a step forwards; stood still; heaved with passion. She was a little startled at the evident force he used to keep himself calm. She was not sure of the nature of the emotions she had provoked. It was only their violence that was clear. His eyes glowed, his figure was dilated, his breath came thick and fast, a ragged percussion that polluted the quiet air. It was a rich, unnamable emulsion of joy, of anger, of pride, of glad surprise, of panting doubt; but she could not parse its complex ingredients. Still it made her uneasy,—as the presence of all strong feeling, of which the root cause is not fully understood or sympathised in, always has this effect. 

She went to the side-board, opened a drawer, and took out a linen duster. She had seen a drop of eau de Cologne on the polished mahogany arm of the sofa. Its sharp, volatile oils of cold-pressed bergamot and bitter orange peel clashed violently with the room’s muted olfactory landscape of beeswax and damp wool, and she instinctively sought to wipe away the stinging scent. But she kept her back turned to her son much longer than was necessary; and when she spoke, her voice seemed unusual and constrained. 

“You have taken some steps about the rioters, I suppose? You don’t apprehend anymore violence, do you? Where were the police? Never at hand when they’re wanted!” 

“On the contrary, I saw three or four of them, when the gates gave way, struggling and beating about in fine fashion; and more came running up just when the yard was clearing. I might have given some of the fellows in charge then, if I had had my wits about me. But there will be no difficulty, plenty of people can identify them.” 

“But won’t they come back to-night?” 

“I’m going to see about a sufficient guard for the premises. I have appointed to meet Captain Hanbury in half an hour at the station.” 

“You must have some tea first.” She offered the ritual not as a mere beverage, but as an ancient, stabilizing cure. The deep, malty liquor of Assam, rich with the history of distant soil, possessed a tannic astringency meant to recalibrate a rattled nervous system. 

“Tea! Yes, I suppose I must. It’s half-past six, and I may be out for some time. Don’t sit up for me, mother.” 

“You expect me to go to bed before I have seen you safe, do you?” 

“Well, perhaps not.” He hesitated for a moment. “But if I’ve time, I shall go round by Crampton, after I’ve arranged with the police and seen Hamper and Clarkson.” 

Their eyes met; they looked at each other intently for a minute. Then she asked: “Why are you going round by Crampton?” 

“To ask after Miss Hale.” 

“I will send. Williams must take the water-bed she came to ask for. He shall inquire how she is.” 

“I must go myself.” 

“Not merely to ask how Miss Hale is?” 

“No, not merely for that. I want to thank her for the way in which she stood between me and the mob.” 

“What made you go down at all? It was putting your head into the lion’s mouth!” He glanced sharply at her; saw that she did not know what had passed between him and Margaret in the drawing-room: and replied by another question: 

“Shall you be afraid to be left without me, until I can get some of the police; or had we better send Williams for them now, and they could be here by the time we have done tea? There’s no time to be lost. I must be off in a quarter of an hour.” 

Mrs. Thornton left the room. Her servants wondered at her directions, usually so sharply-cut and decided, now confused and uncertain, lacking their typical crisp articulation. Mr. Thornton remained in the dining-room, trying to think of the business he had to do at the police-office, and in reality thinking of Margaret. Everything seemed dim and vague beyond—behind—besides the phantom weight of her arms round his neck, the soft clinging which made the dark colour come and go in his cheek as he thought of it. 

The consumption of the tea would have been a silent, restorative communion, but for Fanny’s perpetual, grating description of her own feelings. Her shallow chatter—how she had been alarmed, and then thought they were gone, and then felt sick and faint—functioned as a kind of domestic noise pollution, a trivial, commercialized dissonance against the deep, resonant truth of the day's near-tragedy. 

“There, that’s enough,” said her brother, rising from the table, his ear weary of the superficial tone. “The reality was enough for me.” 

He was going to leave the room, when his mother stopped him with her hand upon his arm. “You will come back here before you go to the Hales’,” said she, in a low, anxious voice that carried the weight of generations. 

“I know what I know,” said Fanny to herself. 

“Why? Will it be too late to disturb them?” 

“John, come back to me for this one evening. It will be late for Mrs. Hale. But that is not it. To-morrow, you will—— Come back to-night, John!” She had seldom pleaded with her son at all—she was too proud for that; but she had never pleaded in vain. 

“I will return straight here after I have done my business. You will be sure to enquire after them?—after her?” 

Mrs. Thornton was by no means a talkative companion to Fanny, nor yet a good listener while her son was absent. But on his return, her eyes and ears were keen to see and to listen to all the details which he could give, as to the steps he had taken to secure himself, and those whom he chose to employ, from any repetition of the day’s outrages. He clearly saw his object. Punishment and suffering were the natural, cyclical consequences to those who had taken part in the riot. All that was necessary, in order that property should be protected, and that the will of the proprietor might cut to his end, clean and sharp as a butcher's blade through cartilage. 

“Mother! You know what I have got to say to Miss Hale, to-morrow?” 

The question came upon her suddenly, during a pause in which she, at least, had forgotten Margaret. She looked up at him. 

“Yes! I do. You can hardly do otherwise.” 

“Do otherwise! I don’t understand you.” 

“I mean that, after allowing her feelings so to overcome her, I consider you bound in honour—” 

“Bound in honour,” said he scornfully, rejecting her translation of the event. “I’m afraid honour has nothing to do with it. ‘Her feelings overcome her!’ What feelings do you mean?” 

“Nay, John, there is no need to be angry. Did she not rush down and cling to you to save you from danger?” 

“She did!” said he. “But, mother,” continued he, stopping short in his walk right in front of her. “I dare not hope. I never was faint-hearted before; but I cannot believe such a creature cares for me.” 

“Don’t be foolish, John. Such a creature! Why, she might be a duke’s daughter, to hear you speak. And what proof more would you have, I wonder, of her caring for you? I can believe she has had a struggle with her aristocratic way of viewing things; but I like her the better for seeing clearly at last. It is a good deal for me to say,” said Mrs. Thornton, smiling slowly, while the salty sting of tears stood in her eyes; “for after to-night, I stand second. It was to have you to myself, all to myself, a few hours longer, that I begged you not to go till to-morrow.” She recognized the circular nature of time—the mother supplanted by the wife, an ancient, agrarian rhythm of winter yielding to an inevitable, albeit bitter, spring harvest. 

“Dearest mother!” (Still love is selfish, and in an instant he reverted to his own hopes and fears in a way that drew the cold creeping shadow over Mrs. Thornton’s heart.) “But I know she does not care for me. I shall put myself at her feet—I must. If it were but one chance in a thousand—or a million—I should do it.” 

“Don’t fear!” said his mother, crushing down her own personal mortification at the little notice he had taken of the rare ebullition of her maternal feelings—of the pang of jealousy that betrayed the intensity of her disregarded love. “Don’t be afraid,” she said, coldly. “As far as love may go she may be worthy of you. It must have taken a good deal to overcome her pride. Don’t be afraid, John,” said she, kissing him, as she wished him good-night. And she went slowly and majestically out of the room. But when she got into her own, she locked the door, and sate down to cry unwonted tears.

* * * * *

Margaret entered the room (where her father and mother still sat, holding low conversation together), looking very pale and white, her skin as blanched as bone ash. She came close up to them before she could trust herself to speak. 

“Mrs. Thornton will send the water-bed, mamma.” 

“Dear, how tired you look! Is it very hot, Margaret?” 

“Very hot, and the streets are rather rough with the strike.” The sonic pollution of the mob—the mechanical roar of human voices stripped of their individual syllables, compressed into a blunt, industrial noise—still rang in her inner ear, deeply unsettling her psychological well-being. Margaret’s colour came back vivid and bright as ever; but it faded away instantly. 

“Here has been a message from Bessy Higgins, asking you to go to her,” said Mrs. Hale. “But I’m sure you look too tired.” 

“Yes!” said Margaret. “I am tired, I cannot go.” 

She was very silent and trembling while she made tea. She sought solace in the precise, culinary ritual, measuring the leaves with a careful hand. The steam rising from the pot carried a ghost of the agrarian south—a faint, floral aroma of damp earth and sun-warmed botanicals that stood in sharp defiance of the sterile, coal-dusted air of Milton. She was thankful to see her father so much occupied with her mother as not to notice her looks. Even after her mother went to bed, he was not content to be absent from her, but undertook to read her to sleep. 

Margaret was alone. 

“Now I will think of it—now I will remember it all. I could not before—I dared not.” 

She sat still in her chair, her hands clasped on her knees, her lips compressed, her eyes fixed as one who sees a vision. She drew a deep breath, inhaling the lingering scent of steeped tea and cold hearth-ash. “I, who hate scenes—I, who have despised people for showing emotion—who have thought them wanting in self-control—I went down and must needs throw myself into the melée, like a romantic fool! Did I do any good? They would have gone away without me, I dare say.” 

But this was overleaping the rational conclusion,—as in an instant her well-poised judgment felt. “No, perhaps they would not. I did some good. But what possessed me to defend that man as if he were a helpless child! Ah!” said she, clenching her hands together, “it is no wonder those people thought I was in love with him, after disgracing myself in that way. I in love—and with him too!” 

Her pale cheeks suddenly became one flame of fire; and she covered her face with her hands. When she took them away, her palms were wet with scalding tears that tasted sharply of salt and iron. “Oh how low I am fallen that they should say that of me! I could not have been so brave for any one else, just because he was so utterly indifferent to me—if, indeed, I do not positively dislike him. It made me the more anxious that there should be fair play on each side; and I could see what fair play was. It was not fair,” said she vehemently, “that he should stand there—sheltered, awaiting the soldiers, who might catch those poor maddened creatures as in a trap—without an effort on his part, to bring them to reason. And it was worse than unfair for them to set on him as they threatened. I would do it again, let who will say what they like of me. If I saved one blow, one cruel, angry action that might otherwise have been committed, I did a woman’s work. Let them insult my maiden pride as they will—I walk pure before God!” 

She chafed at the untranslatability of her own heart. The dominant culture of the masters would parse her complex, almost mythic drive to protect life into the narrow, suffocating lexicon of female swooning and romantic devotion, eroding the profound, historical memory of what it meant to stand as an agrarian peacemaker amidst industrial slaughter. But as she reconciled her truth, she looked up, and a noble peace seemed to descend and calm her face, till it was “stiller than chiselled marble.” 

Dixon came in: “If you please, Miss Margaret, here’s the water-bed from Mrs. Thornton’s. It’s too late for to-night, I’m afraid, for missus is nearly asleep: but it will do nicely for to-morrow.” 

“Very,” said Margaret. “You must send our best thanks.” 

Dixon left the room for a moment. “If you please, Miss Margaret, he says he’s to ask particular how you are. I think he must mean missus; but he says his last words were, to ask how Miss Hale was.” 

“Me!” said Margaret, drawing herself up. “I am quite well. Tell him I am perfectly well.” But her complexion was as deadly white as milled flour; and her head ached intensely, a throbbing reminder of the day’s acoustic violence. 

Mr. Hale now came in. He had left his sleeping wife; and wanted, as Margaret saw, to be amused and interested by something that she was to tell him. With sweet patience did she bear her pain, without a word of complaint, rummaging up numberless small subjects for conversation—offering conversational morsels like a meticulous food historian carefully selecting preserved winter fruits, providing gentle sustenance while keeping the toxic, rotting noise of the riot locked away. She never named it once. It turned her sick to think of it. 

“Good-night, Margaret. I have every chance of a good night myself, and you are looking very pale with your watching. I shall call Dixon if your mother needs anything. Do you go to bed and sleep like a top; for I’m sure you need it, poor child!” 

“Good-night, papa!” 

She let her colour go—the false smile fade away—the eyes grow dull with heavy pain. She released her strong will from its laborious task. Till morning she might feel ill and weary. She lay down and never stirred. To move hand or foot, or even so much as one finger, would have been beyond the powers of either volition or motion. She was so tired, so stunned, that she thought she never slept at all; her feverish thoughts passed and repassed the boundary between sleeping and waking, pacing in an endless, circular loop, keeping their own miserable identity. She could not be alone, prostrate, powerless as she was,—a cloud of faces looked up at her, giving her no idea of fierce vivid anger, or of personal danger, but a deep sense of shame that she should thus be the object of universal regard—a sense of shame so acute that it seemed as if she would fain have burrowed into the earth to hide herself, and yet she could not escape out of that unwinking glare of many eyes.

The next morning, Margaret dragged herself from the heavy linens, thankful that the stifling night had finally run its course—unrefreshed, yet rested. Time, she often felt, did not march forward in the relentless, linear rhythm of the Milton looms, but rather circled back upon itself, much like the ancient agricultural calendars of her ancestors in Helstone. As she moved to the kitchen to stir a restorative morning broth of roasted bone marrow, winter savory, and wild nettle for her mother, Margaret closed her eyes. She inhaled the rich, herbaceous steam, letting the deep, gustatory warmth anchor her. It was a culinary ritual, a quiet, mythic act of sustenance woven seamlessly into the mundane morning. Through the windowpanes vibrated the mechanized, soul-shattering clatter of Milton’s industrial noise pollution, a stark contrast to the acoustic harmony she held in her cultural memory: the restorative symphony of rushing wind through thick green woods, a pleasant, murmuring dance that had always shaped the psychological well-being of her youth. 

She sat at her needlework in Mrs. Hale’s room. As soon as that forenoon slumber was over, she would help her mother to dress; after a dinner of braised root vegetables and salted mutton, she would go and see Bessy Higgins. She would banish all recollection of the Thornton family—no need to think of them till they absolutely stood before her in flesh and blood. But the effort not to think of them brought them only the more strongly before her; and from time to time, the hot flush came over her pale face, sudden as a blast of heat from an open hearth oven.

Dixon opened the door very softly, her stealthy tiptoeing briefly altering the room’s fragile acoustic ecology, and stole up to Margaret by the shaded window. 

“Mr. Thornton, Miss Margaret. He is in the drawing-room.”

Margaret dropped her sewing, the scent of the dried lavender pouch in her lap wafting upward. “Did he ask for me? Isn’t papa come in?”

“He asked for you, miss; and master is out.”

“Very well, I will come,” said Margaret, quietly. But she lingered strangely, grounding herself in the tactile sensation of her needle and thread.

Downstairs, Mr. Thornton stood by one of the windows, his back to the door, immersed in the unrelenting, discordant hum of the commercial city he had helped build. But, in truth, he was deaf to it; he was afraid of himself. His heart beat thick at the thought of her coming, a heavy, primitive percussion. He could not forget the tactile shock of her arms around his neck. The recollection of her clinging defence seemed to thrill him through and through, melting away every resolution and all power of self-control, as easily as clarified butter liquefies in a hot copper pan. He dreaded lest he should go forwards to meet her, with his arms held out in mute entreaty that she would nestle there once more. His heart throbbed loud and quick. Strong man as he was, he trembled at the inherent untranslatability of his profoundest subjective experience. The dominant lexicon of commerce and industry offered him no vocabulary for this terrifying vulnerability. One moment he glowed with impatience, the next he feared a passionate rejection—a blight that would wither up his future like a late frost on a spring crop. 

He was startled by a shift in the room’s sonic landscape. She had come in so gently that he had never heard her; the harsh, acoustic pollution of the street had masked her slow movements in her soft muslin gown. She stood by the mahogany table, smelling faintly of clean starch and the dried rosemary with which her garments were pressed. Her eyelids were dropped half over her eyes; her teeth were shut, her lips just parted. Her slow deep breathings dilated her beautiful nostrils, the only motion visible on her countenance. Her fine-grained skin, resembling freshly milled flour, and the rich outline of her mouth, were wan and pale to-day. The heavy shadow of her dark hair was brought down upon her temples to hide all sign of the blow she had received. Her head was thrown a little back, in the old proud attitude of her agrarian forebears. Altogether she looked like some prisoner, falsely accused of a crime she loathed, too indignant to justify herself. 

Mr. Thornton made a hasty step forwards, recovered himself, and went with quiet firmness to the door to shut it, abruptly cutting off the roar of the street. In the sudden, ringing silence, he came back and stood opposite her.

“Miss Hale, I was very ungrateful yesterday—”

“You had nothing to be grateful for,” said she, raising her eyes. “You mean, I suppose, that you believe you ought to thank me for what I did.” 

In spite of herself, the thick blushes rushed over her face, hot as a baker’s hearth, and burnt into her very eyes. 

“It was only a natural instinct; any woman would have done just the same. We all feel the sanctity of our sex as a high privilege when we see danger. I ought rather,” said she, hastily, “to apologise to you, for having said thoughtless words which sent you down into the danger.”

“It was not your words; it was the truth they conveyed, pungently as it was expressed,” he said, trying to bridge the linguistic gap between his Northern industrial dialect and her Southern sensibilities. “But you shall not drive me off upon that, and so escape the expression of my deep gratitude, my—” He was on the verge now. He would not speak in the haste of his hot passion; he would weigh each word. 

“I do not try to escape from anything,” said she, her voice steady, asserting her own linguistic power. “I simply say, that you owe me no gratitude; and I may add, that any expression of it will be painful to me, because I do not feel that I deserve it. Still, if it will relieve you from even a fancied obligation, speak on.”

“I do not want to be relieved from any obligation,” said he, goaded by her calm manner. “Fancied, or not fancied—I question not myself to know which—I choose to believe that I owe my very life to you—ay—smile, and think it an exaggeration if you will. I believe it, because it adds a value to that life to think—oh, Miss Hale!” he continued, lowering his voice to a tender, resonant timbre that altered the very air pressure in the room, “to think circumstances so wrought, that whenever I exult in existence henceforward, I may say to myself, ‘All this gladness in life, all honest pride in doing my work in the world, all this keen sense of being, I owe to her!’ And it doubles the gladness, it sharpens the sense of existence till I hardly know if it is pain or pleasure to think that I owe it to one—nay, you must, you shall hear—to one whom I love, as I do not believe man ever loved woman before.”

He held her hand tight in his. He panted as he listened for what should come, a jarring noise against the quiet. She threw the hand away with fierce indignation, her words falling like ice crystals into a warm broth. 

“Your way of speaking shocks me. It is blasphemous. I cannot help it, if that is my first feeling. It might not be so, I dare say, if I understood the kind of feeling you describe. I do not want to vex you; and besides, we must speak gently, for mamma is asleep; but your whole manner offends me—”

“How!” exclaimed he. “Offends you! I am indeed most unfortunate.”

“Yes!” said she with recovered dignity, her voice steeped in the ancestral pride of generations who knew the profound power dynamics embedded in how a thing is named. “I do feel offended; and, I think, justly. You seem to fancy that my conduct of yesterday was a personal act between you and me; and that you may come and thank me for it, instead of perceiving, as a gentleman would—yes! a gentleman,” she repeated, invoking that ancient, eroding word from her native lexicon, pregnant with cultural memory, “that any woman, worthy of the name of woman, would come forward to shield, with her reverenced helplessness, a man in danger from the violence of numbers.”

“And the gentleman thus rescued is forbidden the relief of thanks!” he broke in contemptuously, his voice cutting like a mill's saw. “I am a man. I claim the right of expressing my feelings.”

“And I yielded to the right; simply saying that you gave me pain by insisting upon it,” she replied, proudly. “But you seem to have imagined, that I was not merely guided by womanly instinct, but”—and here the passionate tears, kept down for long, came up into her eyes and choked her voice—“but that I was prompted by some particular feeling for you—you! Why, there was not a man—not a poor desperate man in all that crowd—for whom I had not more sympathy—for whom I should not have done what little I could more heartily.”

“You may speak on, Miss Hale. I am aware of these misplaced sympathies of yours. I now believe that it was only your innate sense of oppression—(yes; I, though a master, may be oppressed)—that made you act so nobly as you did. I know you despise me; allow me to say, it is because you do not understand me.”

“I do not care to understand,” she replied, taking hold of the table to steady herself, grounding her hands against its polished, tactile grain. She thought him cruel, and she was weak with her indignation.

“No, I see you do not. You are unfair and unjust.”

Margaret compressed her lips. She would not speak in answer to such accusations. But, for all his savage words, he could have thrown himself at her feet, and kissed the hem of her garment. She did not speak; she did not move. The tears of wounded pride fell hot and fast. He waited awhile, longing for her to say something, but she was silent. The space between them stretched, thick and heavy, punctuated only by the distant, unrelenting commercial roar of Milton—a sound that ceaselessly eroded the quiet spaces necessary for human communion.

He took up his hat. “One word more. You look as if you thought it tainted you to be loved by me. You cannot avoid it. Nay, I, if I would, cannot cleanse you from it. But I would not if I could. I have never loved any woman before: my life has been too busy, my thoughts too much absorbed with other things. Now I love, and will love. But do not be afraid of too much expression on my part.”

“I am not afraid,” she replied, lifting herself straight up. “No one yet has ever dared to be impertinent to me, and no one ever shall. But, Mr. Thornton, you have been very kind to my father,” said she, changing her tone to a most womanly softness, seeking a fragile linguistic bridge. “Don’t let us go on making each other angry. Pray don’t!”

He took no notice of her words. He occupied himself in smoothing the nap of his hat with his coat-sleeve, and then, rejecting her offered hand, and making as if he did not see her grave look of regret, he turned abruptly away and left the room. 

Margaret caught one glance at his face before he went. When he was gone she thought she had seen the gleam of washed tears in his eyes; and that turned her proud dislike into a sharp, painful self-reproach. Her mind circled back to the quiet kitchens of her ancestors, where conflicts were smoothed over the shared rituals of breaking bread, not fractured by the blunt, inadequate lexicon of industrial pride. 

“But how could I help it?” she asked herself, inhaling the faint, lingering scent of his woolen coat that still hung in the air. “I never liked him. I was civil; but I took no trouble to conceal my indifference. Indeed, I never thought about myself or him, so my manners must have shown the truth. All that yesterday, he might mistake. But that is his fault, not mine. I would do it again, if need were, though it does lead me into all this shame and trouble.” 

She stood alone in the ringing silence of the house, craving only the grounding labor of the kitchen, the familiar smell of rising dough, and the restorative hum of a simmering kettle to wash away the bitter aftertaste of a language she could not speak.

The reverberations of Mr. Thornton’s voice still clung to the drawing-room walls, an abrasive acoustic residue that seemed to vibrate against the delicate teacups and the polished oak. Margaret stood entirely still, attempting to process the sudden, violent rupture in her sonic environment. His proposal had not been an offering; it had been an industrial intrusion, a dominant lexicon of possession and rock-like power that sought to override the quiet, subjective dialect of her own heart. 

She retreated to the kitchen, seeking the profound, grounding rituals of sustenance to steady her trembling hands. As she automatically began to feed the hearth fire, the scent of woodsmoke mingled with the lingering olfactory memory of Helstone—a stark contrast to the sterile, suffocating coal-ash of Milton. She compared Henry Lennox’s proposal to Thornton’s. Lennox had offered a polite, softly spoken Southern idiom that momentarily slid over the boundary of friendship before retreating gracefully. Thornton’s declaration, however, lacked any preceding grammar of intimacy. They had shared no shared dialect of friendship, only a continuous clash of untranslatable experiences. His booming, passionate cadences had dismissed her meticulous, agrarian values as though they were mere chaff, attempting to force her into submission through the sheer volume of his will. Yet, beneath the deafening clangor of his pride, the clear conviction dawned on her, tasting as sudden and sharp as bitter almond on the tongue: he did love her. The realization sent a shudder through her, a mythic weight settling into the everyday rhythm of her domestic duties. She disliked him all the more for having invaded her inner acoustic sanctuary. 

Needing to dispel the oppressive atmosphere, she prepared a basket for Bessy Higgins. Into it, she placed a jar of calf’s-foot jelly she had meticulously clarified that morning. She had steeped it in lemon peel, star anise, and a whisper of elderflower—a recipe passed down through generations of Hampshire women, a culinary artifact preserving a fading cultural memory. 

Stepping out into the noonday heat, the mechanical roar of Milton immediately assaulted her. The relentless thumping of the looms and the shrieking of factory whistles formed a wall of industrial noise pollution that Margaret felt as a physical weight, a stark reminder of the commercialization of human labor that crushed the spirit. 

When she reached the Higgins’ home, the air was thick and stagnant. Bessy lay flat upon the settle, moved close to the fire despite the sultry heat. She looked entirely drained, a victim not merely of the cotton fluff in her lungs, but of the ceaseless auditory violence of her world. Margaret, moving with the silent, instinctive grace of a caregiver attuned to the physical needs of others, gently elevated Bessy’s pillows to open her airways, offering a spoonful of the tart, aromatic jelly to soothe her parched throat.

"I thought I should na’ ha’ seen yo’ again," Bessy whispered, her voice a fragile, dying dialect against the distant rumble of the mills.

"I could not come yesterday. My mother was so ill," Margaret replied softly, her own voice modulated to offer acoustic comfort. 

"The wraglin’ and the loud voices had just torn me to pieces," Bessy confessed, her eyes wide with the trauma of the sonic chaos. "I thought when father left, oh! if I could just hear her voice, reading me some words o’ peace and promise, I could die away into the silence and rest o’ God, just as a baby is hushed up to sleep."

Bessy’s plea for auditory relief resonated deeply with Margaret. But before Margaret could read, Bessy’s mind returned to the discordant events of the riot. She explained how her father, Nicholas Higgins—a man holding fast to the traditional, agrarian-rooted principles of collective honor and measured resistance—had seen his careful work destroyed. The Union had demanded a silent, steadfast strike, a refusal to engage in the chaotic noise of violence. But Boucher, driven by starvation and lacking the discipline of the elders, had shattered that silence.

"Committee didn’t want to have right all mixed up wi’ wrong," Bessy explained, struggling for breath, "no more nor I can separate the physic-powder from th’ jelly yo’ gave me to mix it in; jelly is much the biggest, but the bitter powder tastes it all through."

She recounted the devastating clash of power dynamics between her father and Boucher. Higgins, recognizing that Boucher’s stone-throwing had corrupted their righteous cause, had threatened to turn him over to the police, attempting to reassert the moral lexicon of the strike. In response, Boucher, weeping in a frenzy of spent passion and hunger, had struck Higgins in the face. Higgins, stunned by the blow and the sight of a starving man turning to brute force, had ultimately refused to surrender him to the authorities, choosing instead the complex, untranslatable mercy of their shared suffering. 

"And now dunnot talk to me, but just read out th’ chapter," Bessy pleaded. "I want some thoughts of the world that’s far away to take the weary taste of it out of my mouth. Read about the New Heavens, and the New Earth."

Margaret’s soft, low voice filled the small, soot-stained room. She spoke the ancient, mythic words with deliberate, rhythmic pacing, allowing the cadence to act as an acoustic balm. As the rich, evocative imagery of a renewed world washed over them, the moisture of tears gathered on Bessy's eyelashes, and she finally slipped into a restless sleep.

Upon returning home, Margaret found her mother in the drawing-room, enveloped in a rare moment of comfort on the new water-bed. The domestic space was hushed, the industrial noise kept at bay by heavy curtains. Mrs. Hale was sipping a restorative, slow-simmered bone broth, rich with the savory depth of roasted marrow and thyme, though her mind was miles away, wandering through the interwoven timelines of her youth.

"It is more like the beds at Sir John Beresford’s than anything I have slept on since," Mrs. Hale mused, the broth warming her fragile frame. "People seem to have lost the art of making such things, just as they have forgotten how to cure the hams or dry the lavender as we did at Oxenham. Do you not find them a perfect repose, Margaret?"

"I am usually so sleepy at night that I nap off directly," Margaret said, smiling gently as she took her mother's hand. 

The mention of Oxenham, however, triggered a profound shift in Mrs. Hale’s demeanor. The culinary and sensory memories of the past seamlessly unlocked the locked vault of her grief. She spoke of Frederick, of his infancy, of the deep, untranslatable bond he shared with Dixon, who had fed him and cared for him when Mrs. Hale was too weak. 

"Everybody loved him," Mrs. Hale said, her voice trembling. "He was born with the gift of winning hearts... and now, Margaret, I don't know where my boy is, and sometimes I think I shall never see him again."

Tears flowed without restraint, a sudden, torrential release of maternal sorrow. Then, sitting stiffly upright, her eyes dilated and solemn, she grasped Margaret’s hand. The fading matriarch, sensing the rapid erosion of her own life, issued a desperate, passionate demand. "If I am to die, Margaret, I must see my child first. I charge you, bring him to me that I may bless him. The longing to see Frederick stands between me and God."

Margaret recognized this not as a mere whim, but as a vital need for spiritual and emotional sustenance. To deny her mother this final connection to their familial bloodline, to the very dialect of their shared past, would be a starvation of the soul. 

"I will write to-night, mamma," Margaret promised, her voice a steady anchor in the swirling emotion. "He shall come."

With swift, urgent strokes, Margaret drafted the letter. The scratch of the pen was the only sound in the room, a desperate attempt to bridge oceans and translate her mother's dying wish into a summons that could defy the British Empire. She sealed it and carried it out into the smog-choked afternoon, hurrying to the post office before the five o'clock collection.

On her way back, the acrid, metallic tang of coal smoke sitting heavy on her tongue, she was overtaken by her father. When she confessed what she had done, Mr. Hale’s face tightened with dread.
